Cf. the contents heading "مقرر کردن منار و چاه و درختان در شاهراه", printed page ۴۵۳ (p. 590).
- **Firuz Shah's building programme:** "مساجد و مقابر و مدارس و خوانق و دارالشفا و رباط…"; "در بعضے تواریخ" the count is "سی شهر و چهل جامع مسجد پخته[OCR?] و سی[OCR?] مدرسه و بست[OCR?] خانقاه و دویست[OCR?] رباط و صد نهر و صد کوشک و صد و پنجاه چاه و ده حمام و پنج دارالشفا و صد مقبره و ده مینار کلان", most still standing "تا حال که زیاده از سیصد سال منقضی میشود" (p. 339).
- **Religious buildings across Hindustan:** "در هر جانب و هر ضلع امصار و بلاد و قصبات و قریات و رباطات و قلعجات مشتمل بر مساجد و معابد و خوانق و صوامع"; such prosperity "در ممالک دیگر … کمتر نشان مے دهند" (p. 580).
- **State works in Lahore:** Aurangzeb's embankment: "درهر سال ترمیم و تعمیر از سرکار بادشاهی میشود و ... مبلغی کلیه بخرج میرود"; the stone mosque cost more than "پنج لک روپیه" (p. 524). The Lahore palace cost "هشت لک[OCR?] روپیه" (p. 135). Mubarak Shah resettled Lahore and ordered "ترمیم و تعمیر قلعه" (p. 327); Shaikh Ali garrisoned Lahore fort with "دوازده هزار سوار انتخابی" (p. 325). "قلعه لاهور" as Khusraw Malik's refuge (p. 404).
- **Forts and towns:** Attock, regnal year 26, two years, under Khwaja Shamsuddin Khwafi (p. 201); Allahabas, completed regnal year 28, with a dam against the Ganga's flood (p. 203); Agra fort founded "در سال دہم جلوس والا[OCR?] مطابق سنہ ۹۷۳[OCR?]", with "چہار ہزار اوستاد کار" a day (p. 235), "ششصت[OCR?] درعہ[OCR?]" high to the battlements, of dressed stone, finished "در عرض ہشت سال" and named Akbarabad (p. 234). Sialkot fort rebuilt (p. 404); Ghori's repair "درسنه پانصد و هشتاد(۲) هجری", later repairs by Man Singh and Safdar Khan (p. 517). Multan's fort had "هر چهار دروازه", and fort and city are distinct: "از بیرون شهر درون قلعه", "از قلعه بشهر" (p. 296). Tughluq built Tughluqabad; Ulugh Khan built a pavilion for the sultan's feast three kos from it (p. 352).
- **Towns founded:** Nurpur at Dhamheri "بنام نور جهان بیگم" (p. 124); Fathabad[OCR?] with a large sarai at the battle site in pargana Nahrwal[OCR?] (p. 144); Jahangirabad: pargana, jagir, palace, tank and minar, at "یک لک[OCR?] و پنجاه هزار[OCR?] روپیه" (p. 135); Aminabad (p. 176).
- **Building dates in Delhi:** Jama Masjid 1060 AH = 24th Shahjahani year (p. 558); Shahjahanabad "۱۰۴۸[OCR?]" (p. 560); Pass 1 gives the other dates in a "Delhi's successive capitals" bullet in another section (pp. 560–561).
- **Mosque from a grain:** Mian Bhuwa built a mosque at Delhi from the proceeds of the moth grain, and it still stood in the author's day (p. 311; story under Legends).
- **Gardens:** at Hasan Abdal (p. 182); at Kabul "متصل شهر", with water brought "از گذر گاه" (p. 306), and the city's "بازار مسقف" (p. 307), both outside Pakistan.

### Crafts, trade, banking, mines and markets

- **Sialkot:** paper ("مانسنگهی", "حریری", "خاصه جهانگیری"), karchikan worth about "لکهه روپیه" a year, and jamdhar, katari and barchhi (p. 517).
- **Gujrat:** swords, jamdhars, karchikan, and horses worth up to "ده هزار روپیه" (p. 514).
- **Salt Range:** salt-ware and the use of "گچ شیرین" (p. 514). Salt mining: waged miners ("الاشه کش[OCR?]") work in tunnels; "کهوهره[OCR?] و کهیوره[OCR?]" yield lakhs of man a year, and the revenue is "بسرکار بادشاهی ضبط" (p. 514).
- **Wazirabad:** sal-timber and boat trade (p. 512). **Jammu:** tin (p. 515).
- **Multan:** the Arab-bred horse trade via Qandahar, plus carpets, shatranji and chhint (p. 528). **Thatta:** pearls, port goods, dried fish and fish oil (p. 530). Goods move by Indus boat from Multan and Bhakkar to Thatta (p. 529). **Patti** horses fetch up to fifteen thousand rupees (p. 523).
- **Grain by river:** grain boats went from Lahore and Multan to the army in Sindh (p. 174).
- **Banjaras:** "جماعة نقله غله که به زبان عرف بنجاره گویند", grain carriers travelling from Gujarat (p. 451).
- **Bahraich market:** goods from the northern hills come in on men's backs and on hill ponies ("اسپ کونت") — gold, copper and lead; musk, "قطاس", honey and "چوک ترش"; hawks "باز و جره و شاهین و باشه"; and more; a gathering of several months (pp. 545–546).
- **Kumaon:** mines of gold, silver, copper, lead, iron, arsenic and verdigris; musk deer, the "گاو قطاس", silkworms, falcons, "اسپ گوت" and white honey (p. 553).
- **Mines generally:** "الماس و یاقوت و طلا و نقره و مس و سرب و اهن و نمک", "که محصول ان بضبط می درآید", but "بیشتر مدار حاصلات بر زراعات است" (p. 578).
- **Banking (p. 564):** sarrafs return deposits without witnesses; they issue a paper "بخط هندی" payable by their agents up to two hundred farsangs away, called "هنڈی", which can be sold on; for a fee they carry merchants' goods safely, called "بیما".
- **Delhi's bazaar** can furnish the royal workshops in one day and equip "صد هزار سپاه" in an hour (p. 559).
- **Tobacco (pp. 135–133):** it came "از جزایر فرنگ"; "حکما و اطبا" used it in medicines and prescribed the smoke for some illnesses; at first little imported, later farmers across Hind and Hindustan grew it "عالم عالم[OCR?]" and "حاصلات آن را بر اجناس دیگر تفوق می بخشیدند[OCR?]"; its price rose ("نرخش[OCR?] گران تر"); all ranks smoked, "صلحا و زهاد" and "فقرا" included, and it became the best thing to offer a guest (p. 135). The emperor forbade it (p. 134); farmans went to governors against its smoking and sale, and offenders were paraded in Lahore and some had their lips cut (p. 133). Contents heading "دربیان تعریف و مذمت تنباکو و منع گشتن آن بموجب حکم والا", printed page ۴۵۴ (p. 590).

### Agriculture, irrigation, famine and food

- **Agriculture and the rains (p. 578):** rain brings more cultivation, cheaper grain and the prosperity of the "الکه(۱)" (editor's footnote variant "پرگنه"); some cultivation is on wells, some land is "اراضی سیلابه", most is "زمین الهی که عبارت از بارانی بوده باشد", so "اکثر مدار بر باران ست"; land is sown "در سالی دو مرتبه بلکه بعضی جا زیاده بران" — the preceding phrase "صلح و سپس ربع" is unclear (see Doubts). "هوائے برسات" runs "از انجام جوزا … تا ابتدائے میزان"; in it the greedy rest and "سیاحان گیتی نورد از ترددها باز مانده بمکانی آسایش می گیرند" (p. 579).
- **Irrigation:** wells with the ox-driven pot-wheel (p. 510); the four Ravi canals near Shahpur (p. 512).
- **Ahmadabad's water:** the notables build cisterns ("بر که") under their houses, lined with lime, to collect rainwater for the whole year (p. 229).
- **Kashmir:** saffron sown at the end of "فرودی" and through "اردی بهشت", one sowing flowering for six years (p. 508). The Dal lake gardens: Kashmiris lay large timbers on the water, bind them with ropes, throw earth on them and plant gardens, making enough land to farm; "بعضے عیار پیشه همچنین زمین را دزدیده می برند", and the saying about stealing a garden and land comes from here (p. 183). Food: "خورش دائمی[OCR?] انها خشکه نرم[OCR?] بے نمک از برنج است"; eating it hot is not the custom; they keep rice cooked at night "درون[OCR?] دیگے[OCR?]" and eat it (p. 183).
- **Produce (p. 578):** "برنج سکهداس سرآمد جمیع اجناس است", favoured by "بادشاهان و امیران و سایر الناس"; fruits peculiar to Hindustan: "کهتهل", "ڈهڑهل", "اناناس", "کیله", "امود(۳)" (editor's footnote variant "انود"), "شریفه", "نارجیل", "کونله و سنگتره" and "کنار"; the wild "کنار صحرائے" "خود را بر همه کس وقف کرده".
- **Flowers and fruits (chunk 046):** a long list of Indian flowers (p. 576); "کیوڑه و کیتکی" singled out (pp. 576–575); "رائے بیل و چنبیلی" used at weddings and births (p. 575); the mango "انبه", its blossom "مولا" and its fruit "انبا" (p. 577); sugarcane, whose product is sent "به ولایت دور دست بطریق تحفه" (p. 577); greens called "ساگ", and the betel leaf "برگ تنبول" praised (pp. 577–576).
- **Grain prices:** under Sikandar "نرخ غلات ارزان" (p. 315); Ala al-Din's prices fixed and unchanged through the reign (p. 362).
- **Famine:** at Bhakkar, boiled bark of the "سرس" tree (p. 171). Under Muhammad Shah: advances from the treasury for cultivation; those who took money and did not sow were killed; "گندم قمیت[OCR?] ادم[OCR?] پیدا کرد برنج بهمسنگ[OCR?] طلا گردید"; the city gates were shut and many died; people fled to Bengal and "اندیا[OCR?]" (p. 346). At Delhi: dearth, hoarders ("ارباب احتکار"), people drowning themselves in the Jamna, and dogs, cats and even human flesh treated as lawful (p. 370).

### Animals, transport and timekeeping

- **Horses:** in some parts of the country "بهتر از اسپان عراقی و عربی" (p. 575).
- **The elephant:** trunk "سه چهار درعه" long, tusk "یک درعه", ivory called "عاج" (p. 575); up to "هشت درعه" broad and "پنج درعه" high (p. 574); one war elephant reckoned by experienced men "برابر هزار سوار جرار" (p. 574); fights run by mahouts and the goad ("کجک") (p. 573); it becomes young after "شصت سال", has a natural span of "یکصد و بست سال", the female carries "هژده ماه", and it breeds only in the wild (p. 573).
- **Rhinoceros and buffalo:** the rhinoceros comes "خال خال" into the hunts of sultans and khans (p. 573); sultans set wild buffalo against lions, "این شکار خاصه هندوستان است"; town buffalo also fight, and the she-buffalo is called "گاؤمیش" (pp. 573–572).
- **Oxen and carts:** the "گاؤ گجراتی" and the carts "بهل", which seats four, and "رتهه", fit for kings (p. 572).
- **Timekeeping:** the "گهڑیال", a copper bowl "دوازده انگشت" high and wide with a small hole, set in a basin of water, fills in one "گهڑی", and a cast brass plate is struck to sound the hour (p. 572); day and night each divided into four "پهر", a پهر not more than "نه گهڑی" and not less than "شش گهڑی" (pp. 572–571); "سیصد و شصت دم" make one گهڑی and "بست و یک هزار و ششصد دم" a day and night; on journeys a sand-glass of two bottles is used (p. 571).

### War, captives and punishment

- **Siege practice:** at Kangra, batteries, a blockade of supplies, and a garrison starved into terms (p. 123); siege works "نقب وساباط وسرکوب" (p. 416).
- **Army reviews and figures, as printed (no computation):** reviews ("عرض لشکر") by "یساولان" and "قورچیان" (p. 413) and by "بخشیان" (p. 412). Timur's review at Samana: the "یسال" six farsangs long, "هر فرسنگ دوازده هزار سوار احاطه میکند پس سوای نوکر هفتاد و دو هزار سوار بوده باشد" (p. 333). Muhammad Shah enrolled "سه لک[OCR?] و هفتاد[OCR?] هزار سوار" (p. 348). Sultan Husain "یک لک سوار و چهل هزار پیاده و چهار صد فیل"; Bahlul "پانزده هزار سوار" (p. 317); Azam Humayun against Gwalior "سی هزار سوار و سیصد زنجیر فیل" (p. 312); Islam Khan of Manikpur "چهل هزار سوار و پانصد زنجیر فیل" (p. 311). One "کهیونی باصطلاح اهل هند" is "بست و یک هزار و سه صد و هفتاد فیل سوار و بقدر همین عرابه سوار [illegible]یک لک و نود و شش هزار و سه صد و سی اسپ و سه لک و هشت هزار و پنجاه پیاده", the total of one "کهوهن" "[illegible]نج[OCR?] لک و چهل و هفت هزار و یک صد و بست نفر"; both armies "نود و هشت [illegible]لک و چهل و هشت هزار و یک صد و شصت نفر آدم سوای فیل و اسپ و شتر" (p. 487); "هفت کهیونی از جانب پاندوان و یازده کهیونی از طرف کوروان که همگی هژده کهیونی" (p. 488). Persian aid: Shah Tahmasp gave his son Sultan Murad with "دوازده ہزار سوار", and Qandahar, once taken, was to be handed to the Shah's men (pp. 280–281).
- **Mahmud's boat fleet:** "چهار هزار کشتی", each with three iron spikes, manned with archers and "بندوق وقاروره" (p. 411).
- **Bounties for heads:** "ہر کس کہ سر مغل از حرام نمکان بیارد یک اشرفی و ہر کہ ہندوستانی بیارد یک دیک[OCR?] روپیہ انعام یابد" (p. 232).
- **Rajput valour:** at Surat the court talked of Rajputs charging onto a two-headed spear; the emperor set his own sword against his belly, and Raja Man Singh struck it away, wounding his hand (pp. 229–230).
- **After battle:** the dead buried, and the wounded, Murad among them, treated by surgeons and physicians (p. 83).
- **Timur's levy and captives:** officers collected "مال امانی" from Delhi, and resistance led to the order for general killing and captivity; "هر لشکرے را حصه پنجاه کس" of captives; the plunder lasted "تا پانزده[OCR?] و روز" (p. 332). Those made homeless "در ایام حکومت او آمده آباد گشتند" under Khizr Khan (p. 328); Delhi lay "خراب مطلق" for two months after Timur left (p. 331).
- **Captives and resettlement:** at the sack of Multan townspeople "از هفت ساله تا هفتاد ساله" were taken captive (p. 294); the inhabitants of Sialkot moved to Bahlulpur (p. 304).
- **The blinding:** Babur had willed that the brothers not seek each other's lives, so "بصلاح دولت خواهان" Kamran was blinded rather than killed and given leave for Mecca (p. 274).
- **Punishment and parading** ("تشهیر") (pp. 378–379). **Prisons:** Bayana's fort formerly held "اسیران عصیان هند"; Gwalior holds "زندانیان مستوجب سیاست" (p. 549).
- **Sikandar's night rounds:** he went about at night in disguise to learn how his amirs and people lived (p. 314).

### Learning, memory, translation and books

- **Sialkot as a "دار العلوم":** teaching dates as printed — from "نهصد و هفتاد و یک هجری" (Kamal al-Din Husain); under Shah Jahan, Abd al-Hakim's madrasa; then his son Abd Allah (p. 516).
- **Tirhut** is "از دیرباز دار العلم و جامع هندی فضیلت" (p. 544); **Banaras** is "معدن فضیلت و جامع فضلائے مدرسه طلبه علم هند" (p. 547).
- **Education:** Farid Khan read Arabic "تا کافیه" and, in Persian, "گلستان و بوستان و سکندر نامه" (p. 273).
- **Translations:** the Mahabharata translated "باهتمام غیاث الدین علی نقیب خان وملا سلطان وعبدالقادر", named "رزم نامه", preface by Abul Fazl (p. 218). The Mahabharata, Harivamsa and Ramayana put into Persian "حسب الحکم" of Akbar, under "باهتمام نقیب خان"; the "جوگ بششت" translated "بموجب امر بادشاهزاده داراشکوه" (p. 583; details under the author's judgements).
- **Memory in song:** Khawas Khan's deeds "تا حال در هندوستان ... در سرود و نغمه می سرایند" (p. 269); Puran Mal's courage "تا حال در اشعار هندیه باکناف[OCR?] گیتی مشهور است" (p. 268).
- **The Akbarnama's third daftar** is described as covering, among other things, "درویشان پرهیزگار و ریاضت کیشان نیکو کار و تفصیل مقابر و مزار و تعریف بتخانه و معابد هر دیار" (pp. 252–253).

### The author, his sources and the editor

- **The author's present:** he says Todar Mal's system is still followed (pp. 179–180); that Jamil Beg's tomb building "تا حال قایم است" (p. 170); that Mian Bhuwa's mosque still stood (p. 311); that most of Firuz Shah's buildings stand "تا حال که زیاده از سیصد سال منقضی میشود" (p. 339); that the Hindus still follow Yudhishthira's way (p. 484); that the Dipaliwal Hindu custodians remain "تا حال" (p. 521). He saw "چهل من گل گلاب بوزن عالم گیری" at Bhaya Devi himself (p. 554). He glosses figures in his own words ("ده هزار بار هزار دام که دولک وپنجاه هزار روپیه بوده باشد", p. 416). He cites "در بعضے تواریخ" for Firuz Shah's building count (p. 339) and "تاریخ فیروز شاهی" for Alauddin's laws (p. 266).
- **Contents headings** quoted for institutions: "دربیان معاف کردن جزیه و طریقه صلح کل ورزیدن", printed page ۳۷۰ (p. 591); "دربیان تعریف و مذمت تنباکو و منع گشتن آن بموجب حکم والا", printed page ۴۵۴ (p. 590); "مقرر کردن منار و چاه و درختان در شاهراه", printed page ۴۵۳ (p. 590).
- **Provenance of the scanned copy:** barcode label "UNIVERSITY OF TORONTO", number 3[OCR?] 1761[OCR?] 00115366[OCR?] 7[OCR?] (p. 1); digitisation stamp "Sri Satguru Jagjit Singh Ji eLibrary / NamdhariElibrary@gmail.com" (p. 1); bookplate "Presented to the LIBRARY of the UNIVERSITY OF TORONTO by Professor Aziz Ahmad", the name in typescript, the arms with the motto VELUT ARBOR[OCR?] ÆVO[OCR?] (p. 2); on the title page a faint handwritten line in Persian/Urdu script under "BY" ([illegible]) and a margin note "1st[OCR?] June[OCR?] 19[illegible]" (p. 7); pencilled call number "DS[OCR?] / 451[OCR?] / S94[OCR?] / 1918[OCR?]" (p. 8); triangular library stamp "LIBRARY / UNIVERSITY / OF T[illegible]", date "JUN 4[OCR?]", "079[OCR?]" (p. 8).
- **Imprint (title page, p. 7):** "EDITED BY M. ZAFAR HASAN, B.A., ASSISTANT SUPERINTENDENT, ARCHÆOLOGICAL SURVEY OF INDIA"; "DELHI / PRINTED AT J. & SONS PRESS / 1918[OCR?]" and "English Title Page printed at I. M. H. P., Delhi"; "To be had of Maulvi Abrar Hasan, Sambhli[OCR?] Gate, Moradabad (U.P.)".
- **The edition's manuscripts, as the editor states:**
  - He first learned of the book from its references in Sir Sayyid Ahmad Khan's *Asar-us-sanadid* (p. 9). W. M. Hailey, Chief Commissioner of Delhi, wrote to the Jaipur Darbar on his behalf; he did not use the Jaipur MSS; Sir John Marshall examined his MSS (p. 9).
  - Five MSS collated; variant readings in the footnotes, the text "marked with the letter "ب[OCR?]"" (p. 10).
  - MS I: Shikasta, partly worm-eaten, "complete and comparatively old and correct"; 273[OCR?] leaves, 35[OCR?]–38[OCR?] lines a page, the first ten leaves recopied; colophon dated in the first regnal year of "Muiz-ud-din Alamgir II", "one thousand one hundred and sixty eight Hijra"; the editor's own copy, bought at Delhi; called "most reliable" because it lacks the added death notice of Aurangzeb (pp. 10–11).
  - MS II: Nastaliq, 505[OCR?] leaves, "Total 63[OCR?] juze … and one leaf"; endorsement names "Sujan Rai Bhandari, the resident of Batala" and dates the copy "circa one thousand two hundred Hijra" under "Abkar, son of … Shah Alam" and Jagat Singh of "Sawai Jaipur"; a last line "Sa[illegible] one thousand eight[OCR?] hundred and sixty four"; the editor states the copyist "seems to have been a Hindu"; bought at Lucknow (pp. 11–12).
  - MS III: Nastaliq, 495[OCR?] leaves, transcription date obliterated; procured at Moradabad through Maulvi Abrar Hasan (p. 12).
  - MS IV: Nastaliq, 282[OCR?] leaves; endorsement calls the author "accomplished in Hindi, Persian and Sanskrit"; lent by Sayyid Ahsan Shah, Rais of Sardhana (p. 12).
  - MS V: Shikasta, incomplete, ending at Shah Jahan's deposition ("see page 490[OCR?]"); 308[OCR?] leaves; belongs to a college whose name is covered by the stamp ([illegible]); lent through "Maulvi Abdul Rahman, the Arabic professor" (pp. 12–13).
  - Three of his MSS carry the added notice of Aurangzeb's death; he translates it from MS II: "Friday, the 28th[OCR?] Zulqada of the year 1118[OCR?] A. H.", "at the age of 91[OCR?] years 17[OCR?] days and 2 hours", at Ahmadnagar (p. 10).
  - Helpers named: Pir Wilayet Shah; Maulvi Ashfaq Ali; the editor's brothers Hafiz Muhammad Hasan, Maqbul Hasan, Fazal Hasan and Bashir Ahmad Hashmi, who helped with the index; his father Maulvi Shariat Ullah; and Maulvi Abrar Hasan. Work began "June 1917", and the editor apologises for lithograph mistakes (p. 15).
- **Editor's footnote variants carried in this section:** "دو کرور" (p. 80); "کنسور داس[OCR?]" (p. 152); the Ajmer, Khandesh and Orissa revenue variants (pp. 534, 536–537, 539); "کرکرنی" (p. 538); "هفتاد دام" (p. 561); "علامت" (p. 579); "پرگنه" and "انود" (p. 578).

### Legends and miracles (karamat)

*Merged from the Legends and miracles sections of all chunks. Each is attributed as the book attributes it ("گویند", "بقول اهل هند" and so on). The book uses the same register for saints, rulers and Hindu tirthas, and all three are kept here. Archive and Pakistani saints and sites come first.*

**Bahauddin Zakariya (Multan)**
- On 7 Safar 660 AH a "نورانی پیری" brought a sealed letter, sent in by his son Sadr al-Din. The Shaikh read it and died, and from the four corners of the house a voice called "دوست بادوست پیوست" (p. 528).

**Shaikh Sadr al-Din, son of Bahauddin Zakariya (Multan)**
- The author explains Prince Muhammad Sultan's death: "مقرر است که هر کس که با درویشان خدا شناس در افتد زود که بنیاد زندگی و دولت او بر افتد". Because Sadr al-Din was "از باریافتگان درگاه ایزدی", "شاهزاده را عداوت شیخ باعث انقطاع رشته بقا گردید", and the prince soon fell in "اندک جنگ" with the Mongols (p. 385).

**Shaikh Rukn al-Din Multani**
- One version of Tughluq's death: the pavilion fell as soon as the Shaikh stood up, after the sultan ignored his warning (p. 351).
- Side by side, the book's "verified" account of the same death gives Nizam al-Din's "هنوز دهلی دور است" (p. 351).
- The other versions are lightning (on the authority of "حاجی محمد قندهاری"), the shock of elephants brought from Bengal, an earthquake, or Ulugh Khan's deliberate building, for which "صدرجهان گجراتی" is cited (pp. 351–352).

**Sultan Sarwar ("سلطان سرور")**
- A Qandahar merchant's camel broke its leg near the "مزار شهید". The merchant vowed a nazr, the leg healed at once and he paid the vow; "این مقوله نادره دراطراف مشهور گردید" (p. 527).
- The blind man, the leper and the impotent man who took up service at the tomb were cured: "نامرد رجولیت و مبروص تندرستی و نابینا بینائے یافت" (p. 527).

**Shaikh Farid Ganjshakar**
- "از برکات نظر کیمیا اثر ایشان تودهاے خاک شکر گردید", hence "گنج شکر" (p. 526).

**Shah Daula**
- The dying Mian Sayyidna's glance changed his state and lit his inner self with gnosis (p. 515).
- "خزاین غیب نمودار مے گشت" (treasures of the unseen became manifest to him). The book offers this as the source of his giving more than he received (p. 515).

**Baba Nanak**
- In his childhood "علامات کشف و کرامات و ایات خارق عادات" appeared (p. 520).

**The Jalaliya rock at Attock**
- The common people say a holy man named "جلالیه" is buried on top of it. The author prefers the Akbar story (pp. 511–510; see the author's judgements below).

**Suba Lahore: the salt range and the Kahyala pool**
- Salt mines: "بحکمت الهی" the tunnels are neither hot in summer nor cold in winter (p. 514).
- Kahyala pool: no one knows its depth. It is the earth's left eye, and the right eye is the pool of "بهکر[OCR?]" near Ajmer. The book reports this as Hindu belief (p. 514).

**Suba Thatta**
- In the Thatta country "بعضے درویشان صاحب حال" saw in dreams that "سکه و خطبه بنام نامی حضرت خدیو جهان درین دیار می شود", and that the plague was due to "سرتابی مردم این ملک" (p. 174).
- The liver-eaters' spells and their cure are reported as fact, with the author's own testimony. This is folk belief, not a saint's karamat (pp. 529–530).

**Suba Kabul (including Peshawar) and the road to it**
- The sand-hill near Ghorband, "خواجه ریگ روان", gives out the sound of drums in summer (p. 505).
- A spring near Ghazni raises cloud and snow if filth falls into it (p. 505).
- A martyr of Chinggis Khan's time lies in his coffin with his body still intact (p. 505).
- The "گنگا" spring at Lohgadh[OCR?]: like the Ganges', its water does not spoil when kept (pp. 505–504).
- Killing a cow or horse on the Pir Panjal raises wind, cloud and snow at once (p. 506).
- Khwaja Tabut at Kabul (p. 143):
  - His body is said to be uncorrupted after four hundred years.
  - A wound on his neck bleeds whenever the cotton is lifted and stops only when it is put back.
  - The book gives this as report ("میگویند", "بعرض رسید").
  - Mu'tamad Khan's inquiry found the body partly intact, but "زخم معلوم نگشت".

**Muslim saints and dervishes outside Pakistan**
- Shaikh Nizamuddin Auliya:
  - Hasan Gangu took the Deccan "بموجب دعاۓ شیخ" (p. 161).
  - The Mongols could not trouble Hindustan in Ala al-Din's time, and "مردم این معنی را از برکات توجهات … شیخ نظام الدین اولیا سے[OCR?] تصور میکردند" (p. 361).
- Shaikh Salim: the son was born "ببرکات دعای[OCR?] [illegible] درویش" (p. 215).
- Qutbuddin Bakhtiyar Kaki: Khizr passed by him when he was young. At eighteen he received khilafat "در عالم خواب" from Muinuddin Chishti, who later came from Ajmer to Delhi "بالهام ربانی" to meet him (p. 556).
- Abu Ali Qalandar: when "جذبه الهی" came he threw all his books into the Jamna, and "بسا خارق عادات ایشان یادگار" (p. 555).
- Abdul Qadir Gilani: his "خوارق عادات و بوارق کرامات" filled the world (p. 555).
- Shah Shams al-Din Daryai (not an archive row) (p. 521):
  - He refused his Hindu servant Dipali leave for the Ganges bath, then told him "چشم بپوش". Dipali found himself at the Ganges and bathed with his kinsmen, who saw him there. He opened his eyes back in the saint's presence.
  - Years after the saint's death, carpenters acting for the "حاکم کلانور" cut a "سرس[OCR?]" tree by the tomb. A terrible noise arose and the ground shook, and the trunk stood up by itself and grew green again.
- Jamil Beg's tomb near Kalanaur: "بحکمت ایزدی مرادات مردم که در آن مکان نذر قبول میکنند بحصول می انجامد" (p. 170).
- Qutb Alam's tomb at Batwa: an object fallen on it is variously taken for wood, stone or iron, and "شگرف داستانی برگذارند" (p. 533).
- The unnamed dervish of Mathura (pp. 132–131):
  - Gold, "قریب هفتصد[OCR?] اشرفی", rained from the air in answer to his and his five disciples' supplication.
  - He promised that the half kept in the treasury "گاهے کمی نخواهد شد".
  - He knew the emperor's unspoken regret about the hand-kiss.
  - Opinion was split ("بعضے ... خوارق[OCR?] بیان میکردند و بعضے بسحر[OCR?] و جادو نسبت میدادند"). The final finding was that it came "از نتایج و فور عبادات و حصول کمالات".
- The dervish of Samana sells the kingship of Delhi (pp. 321–320):
  - He asked "از شما کسی هست که بادشاهی دهلی بدو هزار تنکه بخرد". Malik Bahlul at once laid down "یکهزار و سیصد تنکه نقد[OCR?]", and the dervish said "سلطنت هندوستان مبارک باشد" (p. 321).
  - Bahlul answered his mocking companions: "اگر این قضیه بوقوع خواهد یافت سودائے مفت است والا خدمت درویش خدا اندیش کردم" (p. 320).
  - The book tells his rise as the working of the dervish's "اشارت" and "بشارت" (p. 320).
- An unnamed dervish's prophecy over the four-year-old Farid Khan: "سبحان الله بادشاه هندوستان بطلب درمی گریه می نماید". Hasan Khan took it as confirming the dream (p. 273).
- Sidi Maula: on the day he was killed, "بحکمت ایزدی دران روز ابر سیاه وباد تند برخاست وطوفان گرد وخاک برامد وعالم تاریک گردید". "ودران سال باران نگردید", and drought and famine followed (pp. 371–370).

**Hindu tirthas, temples and deities**
- Jwalamukhi:
  - "از عجایب قدرت قادر حقیقی است که از آغاز آفرینش خود بخود آتش شعله افروز میگردد". Digging and water put nothing out (pp. 121–122).
  - The sultan judged the eternal flame, which water would not quench, a sign of divine power (p. 341).
  - Fire comes out of the ground (p. 517). Outside Pakistan.
- Kangra:
  - Local people point out the umbrella over the idol as standing there "از زمان پاندوان" ("نشان میدهند") (p. 122).
  - "اعتقاد مردم آن دیار آنست" that the fort never passed from one people to another and no outsider ever held it (p. 123).
- Bhawan temple and Nagarkot:
  - "بعضے گنگ مادرزا[OCR?] نوک زبان خود بریده پایان دیوار معبد شبانگاه بر زمین میخوابند و بقدرت الهی روز دیگر زبان انها درست می شود". Some cut off their own heads "و باز درست می گردد", and "مرادات مردم بحصول می انجامد" (p. 342).
  - A tongue cut off as a vow grows back in hours or days. When companions set back the heads of those who cut them off, they "بحکمت الهی از سر نو زندگی یابند" (pp. 518–517). Outside Pakistan.
- Achal near Batala: some of the sick are cured "بیامن دعاے خدا اندیشان" (p. 518).
- Hardwar: the belief that casting bones into the Ganges brings "رستگاری فرورفتگان" is attributed to "اهل هند" (p. 331).
- Somnath (p. 413):
  - "گویند": "درزمان پیغمبر ان بت را ازخانه کعبه براورده درانجا گذاشته اند".
  - The idol has been worshipped since Krishna's time, "چهار هزار سال" ago, as "کتب سلف اهل هند" say.
- Thanesar and Kurukshetra:
  - "باعتقاد اهل هند" creation began at the pool (p. 417).
  - "بقول دانشمندان هند" Brahma came into being there "از گل نیلوفر" and created the world from that place.
  - "اعتقاد ان جماعت" is that any holy person who dies there is freed from "تناسخ" and gains paradise, which is why the site was chosen for the battle (p. 491).
  - It is called "ناف زمین" in Hindu books (p. 554).
- Krishna dies from a hunter's arrow under the pipal on the Sarasvati, half a kos from Somnath. The book states this without "گویند" (p. 532).
- "مشهور است که", some "قریب پنجهزار سال" ago, "پنجاه و شش کرور ادم از قوم جادوان" sank between the Sarasvati and the Hiran (p. 532).
- At Mahadev's temple at Mul a creature dies each year and foretells the rains (pp. 531–532).
- Baijnath (p. 544):
  - A temple servant in need fasts under the pipal and prays to Mahadev. After two or three days a leaf falls, "مخطوط بخط هندی رقم زده کلک غیب": a draft for a sum on some person "هر چند که مسکن ان پانصد کروه باشد", naming him, his family and his home.
  - The temple writes it out as a "هندوی بیجناتهه", and the person named pays "بلا عذر و حیله".
  - Once a year, on "شیوراتت یعنی شیوبرت", earth is brought out of the cave, and "بقدر نصیب هر کدام ازان خاک طلا می شود".
- Tirhut: a milk-seller who waters his milk suffers "از غیب گزندی" (p. 544).
- Nimkhar (p. 545):
  - Brahma's pool boils so that no one can sink in it.
  - Hindu books lost to time came to light again on its bank.
  - At a nearby spring, while Brahmins chant, "از ریگ پیکر مهادیو پدید آید" and soon vanishes.
  - At "چرمتی" fire kindles itself in the heat.
- Bilgram: whoever drinks from a certain well for forty days gains understanding and comeliness (p. 545).
- Prag: the tree Jahangir had cut grew up through the iron plate "بحکمت ایزدی" (p. 548).
- Banaras: when Jupiter enters Leo a small hill rises from the Ganga for a month, "نموداریست از بدایع قدرت ایزدی" (p. 547).
- Kalinjar: "معبدگاه بهیرون که شگرف داستانها ازان برگذارند" (p. 547).
- Ajodhya: Rama's bridge, his armies of monkeys and bears, and Ravan (p. 546).
- The Ganga:
  - By Hindu belief it descends "از بهشت" by Kailas and through China (p. 552).
  - Firdausi's Shahnameh is cited for Siyavush's stone buildings on the Ganga, and the Pandavs dissolved their bodies at "هماچل(۱)" (p. 552).
  - Its water never spoils, even if kept for years, and it heals the sick and strengthens the healthy (p. 551).
  - The people at the river's head and mouth are robbers and rebels. Travellers explain this by the sins washed off in the river being reborn there "بطریق تناسخ" (p. 550).
- Other rivers (p. 543):
  - The Gandak's water swells the throat ("جاغور"), and saligram stones are found within forty kos of it.
  - The Karamnasa is shunned, lest a drop touch the body.
  - "گویند که هفتاد و دو دریائے کشتی رو" join the Ganga by Patna (p. 543; repeated p. 551).
- Ujjain: the Shipra surges with milk, "بقدرت الهی بارها چنین بظهور امده". At Mandu, the dome of Sultan Mahmud son of Hoshang oozes water in summer (p. 536).
- Kutch's Arab horses descend from a wrecked merchant's hundred horses, "گویند" (p. 531).
- Alexander's ("سلطان سکندر رومی") talismanic hand on the sea, and the sorcery of Kamrup: "شگرف داستانها برگذارند" (pp. 540–541).
- Sambhal: "گویند" that the last manifestation of divine light will appear from its Har Mandir (p. 553).
- Tibet: "گویند" that stones with the properties of "پارس" turn grazing animals' iron shoes to gold, and its ruler's vessels and drums are all of gold (p. 553).
- Elephant and human bones dug up on the canal "سلیمه[OCR?]" were held to come from "جنگ پاندوان و کوروان" (p. 343).
- Kashmir, as the book attributes (pp. 508–507):
  - The ice image of Mahadev waxes and wanes.
  - Unseen worship-music is heard at Nausar[OCR?].
  - The Durga temple moves on "اشتمی[OCR?]".
  - Lepers are cured at Baruwa[OCR?].
  - The ground at Sohim[OCR?] is hot enough to cook "چون مشتری باسد رسد".
  - The Kotahar[OCR?] spring has been dry for eleven years.
  - The Shukarnag[OCR?] spring flows only on a Friday falling on the 9th.
  - Divination is practised at Deosar[OCR?] and Pahnal[OCR?].
- More Kashmir springs (p. 509):
  - Near "سنده براری[OCR?]" a spring is dry for six months. When farmers pray and sacrifice sheep and goats on a fixed day, "بقدرت الهی اب بر جوشد" and waters five villages.
  - The water of "گوگرناگ[OCR?]" fills the hungry and makes the full hungry.
- A tablet in Kashmir's Mahadev temple foretold its destruction by one named Sikandar after "یکهزار و یکصد سال" (p. 192).
- On the "کپرتل[OCR?]" road, a drum sounded at the tank of the ancient sages brings snow and rain. When Qasim Khan's army sounded its drum, snow, rain and hail fell, the cold struck the troops, and many animals died (p. 186).
- Kashmir's air: perhaps "مسیحا[OCR?]" was raised there and Khizr nourished in its water and air, so that "عمر ابد[OCR?]" is his lot. This is a rhetorical figure, not a claim (p. 184).

**Jogis, Sannyasis and the powers of the sciences**
- Jogis (p. 567). Most of them, "بقوت ریاضت":
  - "بهوا می پرند و بر سطح دریا می خرامند";
  - put their soul into another body;
  - change their form and turn ash into gold;
  - command "عالم اجنه";
  - know the secrets of hearts.
  - Some Sannyasis can do the same.
- The master of "پاتنجل شاستر", through control of breath, flies on the air, walks on the river, knows men's thoughts, and tells past, present and future (p. 570).
- The Veda came from Brahma, who appeared from a lotus on the primal water (p. 571).
- "سیس ناگ" carries the earth, "بقول اهل هند" (p. 569).
- Masters of archery draw many arrows from one "بقوت علم". Nat performers cut a man limb from limb and restore him "بهمان زمان" (p. 568).
- The preface (pp. 587–586):
  - In every land God clothed one of His elect in human form.
  - Each was "عارف دقایق عقول و نفوس", informed the world of "اخبار ماضی و حال و استقبال", was "مظهر معجزات عجیبه و کرامات غریبه", and bore a "کتاب آسمانی" "بالهام ربانی" (p. 587).
  - His followers count repeating "اعجاز کرامتش" "محض سعادت و عین عبادت" (p. 586).
- Powers attained "بذرایع بدایع علوم" (p. 585):
  - walking on the sea and flying on the air;
  - freeing the heart by "حبس نفس";
  - moving the soul into another body by "خلع بدن";
  - changing form by "سیمیا", and making gold from ash by "کیمیا";
  - subduing "عالم اجنه" by spells;
  - knowing men's secret thoughts by magic;
  - healing the sick.
- A jogi came to the dying Sultan Zain al-Abidin and, "بعلم خلع بدن که میدانست", put his soul into the sultan's body. The courtiers saw the sultan well and the jogi dead, and the body ruled on, "و ازین جهت[OCR?] رعایت دین هندوان کرده" (pp. 191–190).
- The jugglers' wonders: the author calls them "سحر سازی", not miracles, and names the art "سیمیا" (p. 125).

**Epic and raja legends (Part 2)**
- Bhartrihari and Bikramajit:
  - Bhartrihari gained eternal life and, "بقول اهل هند", still wanders hidden in the world (p. 452).
  - Bikramajit understood a jackal's prophecy of kingship because he knew the tongues of animals (p. 450).
  - He overcame the man-eating demon, who became his ally on call (p. 449).
  - The throne's thirty-two figures speak to Raja Bhoj (p. 445).
  - Samandarpal transfers his soul into the raja's body; the author rejects this (p. 443).
- Gandharb Sen (attributed to the "ترجمه سنگهاسن بتیسی"):
  - Indra's curse makes him a donkey by day and a man by night, until a raja burns the donkey body (p. 457).
  - The iron fort round Dharanagri is raised in one night "بدون وساطت معمار و حداد" after his prayer (p. 456).
  - He prophesies Bharthari and a Bikramajit with "قوت هزار فیل", and ascends "بجانب آسمان" (p. 455).
  - Bikramajit is born alive from his dead mother's cut belly (p. 455).
  - The ascetic's fruit "که از خوردن آن زندگانی جاودانی حاصل گردد" (p. 453).
- Parikshit and Janamejaya:
  - The physician restores a tree burned to ash by "فسونی که اعجاز مسیحائی" (p. 475).
  - The serpent takes human form, then the form of a tiny worm hidden in a fruit (pp. 473–475).
  - The snakes are drawn into the sacrificial fire by spells. Some have horses' faces, elephants' trunks or two to four heads, some are one to three kos long, and their fat runs in streams (p. 471).
- Vyasa:
  - His mother was born of a fish that swallowed a raja's seed spilled over the Jamuna (pp. 482–483).
  - Parashar raised "ابر سیاه" by "افسونی". The boy was born at once and became like a fourteen-year-old, the mother's maidenhood stayed intact and her fish smell turned sweet, all within "یکپاس[OCR?]" (p. 482).
  - "آن جماعت" hold that he "خلعت هستی جاوید بدوش داشته تا حال در عالم علوی و سفلی سیار است", and he knew past, future and present (p. 481).
- The hermit's son "سرنگی[OCR?]" was born "از تخم این زاهد و شکم آهو ماده" with a horn on his head, "که این قصه مشهور است". He prayed that whoever hung the snake on his father be bitten "بعد هفت روز" by the "بهجنگ(۲)[OCR?] مار" (editor's footnote variant "تچهک[OCR?]"), and the prayer "مستجاب گشت" (p. 476).
- Yudhishthira did not melt in the snow and went to paradise in his body (pp. 478–479).
- The treasure parable: before Kaliyuga a house's buyer and seller each refused the treasure found under it; after it each claimed it, and Yudhishthira took this as the sign of the age (pp. 479–480).
- Draupadi prayed to "ایزد تعالی که ستار العیوب است", and cloth kept appearing on her as Dushasan pulled it away (p. 493).
- Omens at the exile ("گویند"), read by the diviners as ruin for Dhritarashtra's sons (pp. 492–493):
  - an earthquake, lightning without cloud, an untimely total eclipse and stars falling by day;
  - jackals in the bazaar and vultures on the gate;
  - lotuses on desert trees and the river running backward;
  - fruit out of season, and animals bearing young of another kind.
- Arjun's arrows split into thousands and made fire, wind, dust and water, and he changed size and vanished in battle (p. 497).
- Headless bodies:
  - "بقول فضلاے هند", where ten thousand are killed one lifeless body dances; in this war most of the dead danced (p. 488).
  - "گویند" that headless bodies rose in Ali Shah and Shahi Khan's battle in Kashmir, as a "قول اهل هند" (p. 191).
  - The same is said of the Kashghar invasion of "سنه ۹۳۹[OCR?]" (p. 188).
- Pandu, Kunti and Duryodhana:
  - The deer that was an ascetic curses Raja Pandu (p. 500).
  - Kunti summons sons by a spell, the rock Bhimsen falls on as a newborn breaks, and a heavenly voice proclaims Arjun (p. 499).
  - Duryodhana comes out of a ghee pot braying like an ass, amid omens. At the Pandavas' acceptance a voice speaks from the unseen and flowers rain down (p. 498).

**Rulers' portents and court marvels (no saint involved)**
- Jahangir's time:
  - The royal elephant's iron chain came out of the Narbada "تمام از طلا". The emperor ascribed it to a stone "که بزبان هندی انرا پارس گویند". Divers searched, "لیکن بهم نرسید و زنجیر دیگر طلا نگشت" (p. 163).
  - "از غرایب": fire broke out in Prince Sultan Murad's Deccan palace on the same day as the court fire (p. 168).
  - Tansen's music is said to have drawn "وحشیان در صحرا و طایران در هوا" (p. 168).
  - The astrologer Jotik[OCR?] Rai foretold Shuja's fall and that it would do him no harm (p. 121).
- Akbar's time:
  - The emperor asked about the verse "کلاه خسروی و تاج شاہی / بہر گل کی رسد" at the Ravi. "قضا را در ہمان روز" Yadgar revolted in Kashmir, though the court knew nothing of it (p. 179).
  - The emperor foretold that "این لولی بچہ بمجرد[OCR?] بر آمدن سہیل کشتہ خواہد شد", and Yadgar was killed "بمجرد[OCR?] بر آمدن ستاره یمانی", as "بر زبان الہام ترجمان رفتہ بود" (p. 178).
  - "از غرایب": when Yadgar struck coin he fell into a shaking fever, and steel flew into his seal-engraver's eye (p. 178).
  - Abul Fazl's omen "از دیوان لسان الغیب": "آن خوش خبر کجاست کزین فتح مژده داد" (p. 178).
- Marvels of the reign (p. 212):
  - Raut Bika, headman of "موضع کمبر[OCR?]", killed by two wounds, was reborn in his kinsman Ram Das's house with the scars, "بطریق تناسخ", and the boy named himself.
  - A blind man read speech "از بغل".
  - A man had "بیست[OCR?] ویک" living sons by one wife.
  - A man with no ears heard.
  - After a two-tailed comet, "در عراق وخراسان فتور عظیم رو داد".
  - Infants reared by mute nurses did not learn speech.
- A dead twin's liver showed "سوراخی مانند زخم تیر", proving grief for a son (p. 211).
- Sher Shah:
  - Hasan Khan gave his wife "تازیانه چند" for telling a good dream, because "بزرگان آزموده کار فرموده اند" that one must not sleep again after one (p. 273).
  - His mother, while carrying him, dreamt the shining moon came into her lap: the author's sign of a fortunate king (p. 274).
  - An astrologer chose the hour, and he took Bengal "بتائیدات الهی" (p. 271).
  - Kings' judgement is "پرتویست از شعشه[OCR?] انوار ایزدی". The emperor saw "چشمان این افغان شوخ مینماید" and ordered his arrest, and Sher Khan sensed it "به تفرس" and escaped (p. 272).
- Akbar as a child:
  - At four he picked out his mother "بے تامل" among many veiled women (p. 280).
  - He was kept on Kabul's battlements by "حفظ و حمایت ایزدی" (p. 279).
- Babur and Humayun: "همان وقت ... انوار[OCR?] صحت و شفا بر چهره نورانی شاهزاده و آثار عارضه در ذات والاحضرت بادشاه نمودار گشت". The book ascribes this "بقدرت قادر علی الاطلاق", and it "باعث تعجب نظارگیان گردید" (p. 293).
- The water-carrier's leather coins: "گویند که از چرم مشک خود دراهم و دنانیر کرده ... رایج گردانید چنانچه اینمعنی تا حال زبان زد مردم است" (p. 287).
- Timur's infant "انوار سرداری و سروری", and his games "محض لشکر کشی و صف آراۓ" (p. 310).
- Jewellers "میگفتند" the diamond came "از خزانه سلطان علاء الدین است که اورا از اولاد راجه بکرماجیت بدست آمده بود", worth "نصف خرچ روزمره ربع مسکون" (p. 300).
- The Lodis:
  - "بارادت الهی" the roof fell and Bahlul's mother died. He was cut from her alive, "ازانجا که سلطنت هندوستان نصیب آن طفل بود" (p. 321).
  - "مردم ظن مے بردند که سلطان بجن آشناست". Others said Sikandar had "چراغے از طلسمات" that showed the jinn, the state of the earth and of other kings, and fetched treasures (p. 314).
  - Mian Bhuwa ("گویند") resowed a grain of "موتہ[OCR?]" from Sikandar for years and built a Delhi mosque from the proceeds (p. 311).
- Khizr Khan and Timur:
  - Timur gave him "بشارت سلطنت", and Khizr Khan credited "برکات توجهات حضرت صاحب قران" (p. 328).
  - "بر زبان وحی ترجمان گذشت که من سلطنت هندوستان بخضرخان بخشیدیم" (p. 331).
  - The verse "ذات پاکش که بود خضر شعار / بکرامات رفت بر سر کار" (p. 327).
  - He is a ruler, not a saint.
- Jalal al-Din's killers met divine retribution, Mahmud Salim by leprosy and Ikhtiyar al-Din by madness, and Ala al-Din's line died out (p. 368).
- Malik Ayaz:
  - Following "مولانا زلالی", the Sultan saw him in a dream, went to Badakhshan "بلباس فقیری", knew him from the form seen "درعالم مثال", and bought him (pp. 408–409).
  - "گویند": he was a Kashmiri prince stolen by kidnappers (p. 410).
- Mahmud of Ghazni:
  - "بمشیت ایزدی همان شب خوفے عظیم بخاطر نندا راه یافت" (p. 414).
  - Filth thrown into a spring brought the snow that destroyed Jaipal's men, told as the Sultan's "تدبیر" (p. 426).
- In Kashmir, the seven-year-old Sultan Muhammad Shah chose a bow over gold and arms, which was read as an omen of greatness (p. 190).

### The author's judgements and interpretations

*The author's own statements, recorded as a primary source. The 1918 editor's remarks and footnotes are kept apart at the end. No date is converted.*

**The author on himself, his purpose and his plan**
- What he calls himself:
  - "هیچمدان" (pp. 583, 581) and "احقر العباد" (p. 580).
  - He gives no name, father, town or patron in pp. 578–591.
- His career: "از عنفوان ظهور صبح شعور" he served the administrators of state and revenue in "منشیگری". Through it and "قلاوزی[OCR?] شوق" he read "اکثر نسخه تواریخ" (p. 583).
- Batala:
  - He writes on Batala and its fair at length "چون زاد و بوم نگارنده این نسخه دل کشاے بتاله است" (p. 518).
  - On its name: "چون بزبان پنجاب مبادله را بتاله گویند" (p. 522).
- Why read history (pp. 584–583). He ranks the occupations of the wise:
  - remembering God amid worldly ties, "دست در کار و دل بایار";
  - then "تسبیح و تهلیل";
  - then "کتب حقیقت و طریقت";
  - then "کتب اخلاق";
  - then history, which is "جام جمشید است و اینه سکندر" (p. 584).
  - Worldly men pass their leisure in "شکار و قمار و رود و سرود و اصغائے لاف و گزاف" (p. 584).
- His plan is a "نسخه مختصری متضمن احوال فرمان روایان ناحیه هندوستان" (p. 581):
  - the rajas from "راجه جدهشتر پاندوان" to "رائ پتهورا که خاتمه سلطنت جماعه هنود اوست", "بطریق اختصار", with "انتقال امر سلطنت از قومی بقومی دیگر";
  - the sultans from "سلطان ناصرالدین سبکتگین که بادی مراسم اسلام در هندوستان از وست" to "محمد اورنگ زیب عالم گیر بادشاه";
  - for each ruler, accession, good qualities or "ابات[OCR?] ذمیمه", justice or oppression, and death and succession.
  - Under Shihab al-Din Ghuri "حکومت راجها و رایان … از هندوستان منقطع گشته" (p. 582).
- Where the Muslim section begins: Ghori ended Hindu rule, but he begins with Sabuktigin "چون در ممالک هند ظهور اسلام از سلطان ناصر الدین است" (p. 428).
- What he leaves out: Iran and Turan, since "این نسخه مشتمل بر حقیقت احوال فرمان روایان هند است" (p. 427).
- On originality (p. 581):
  - "عبارات و استعارات این نسخه بدیعه را از کتب دیگران دزدی نه کرده بقدر لیاقت و استعداد خویش بتسطیر دراورده".
  - Some verses are his own, from "طبع ناقص"; most are by famous poets.
  - He revised it "دو سه مرتبه" and finished it "در مدت دو سال".
- The date of composition, in five eras, unflagged and not converted (p. 581):
  - "در سنه چهلم عالم گیری";
  - "مطابق یک هزار و یکصد و هفتم هجری";
  - "هزار و هفتصد و پنجاه و سه سنه بکرماجیت";
  - "هزار و ششصد و هژدهم سنه سالباهن";
  - "چهار هزار و هفتصد و نود و هفت گذشت کلجگ";
  - verb "مرتب گردانید".
- His apology: no man is free of error. He asks the munshis to correct, not mock, if "در سنه و احوال و فرمان روائ تفاوت ظاهر شود" (pp. 581–580).
- His present, as he marks it:
  - "اکنون[OCR?] که نسخه تحریری[OCR?] می در آید", the child born then "زیب اورنگ خلافت هندوستان است" (p. 136).
  - The Deccan houses still rule "تا حال که این نسخه تحریر[OCR?] می درآید" (pp. 158, 159).
  - Jamil Beg's tomb "تا حال قایم است" (p. 170).
  - Aminabad was founded "درین ولا[OCR?]" (p. 176).
  - Todar Mal's rules are still in force "تا حال" (pp. 179–180).
- His own reckonings, quoted and not computed:
  - Since Yudhishthira's accession: "تا حال چهار هزار و هشت صد و سی و شش سال می گذرد" (p. 484).
  - Since the war, by context: "لغایت تسوید این نسخه چهار هزار و هشت صد و سی و پنج سال منقضی می شود". The war is still a byword, and "آثار استخوان و امثال آن دران مرز معاینه [illegible]گردد" (p. 487).
  - Since Abdul Qadir Gilani's death: "از رحلت ایشان لغایت این نسخه پانصد و پنجاه سال منقضی میشود" (p. 555).
  - Kabul's founding, "بموجب تواریخ", reckoned from the writing of "این نسخه" (p. 506).

**His sources and how he weighs them**
- The source list (pp. 583–581; the numbering is Pass 1's). The descriptions are his:
  1. "کتاب رزم نامه ترجمه مهابهارت", "از تواریخ هند بزرگ تر و معتبر تر", on "پاندوان و کوروان و اسلاف انها". By Akbar's order "مولانای عبدالقادر بدایونی و شیخ محمد سلطان تهانیسری" translated it "باهتمام نقیب خان که سرامد تاریخ دانان بود", and "شیخ ابوالفضل" wrote its "خطبه" (p. 583).
  2. "ترجمه هربنس پران", by "مولانائے تبریزی", by Akbar's order (p. 583).
  3. "ترجمه رامائن", by "جماعه مذکور" (p. 583).
  4. "ترجمه بهاگوت[OCR?] جوگ بششت", by "شیخ احمد و دیگر فضلا", by Dara Shikoh's order (p. 583).
  5. "نسخه گل افشان ترجمه سنگاسن بتیسی", on Bikramajit, "که مخترع آن برج[OCR?] پندت وزیر راجه بهوج است" (p. 583).
  6. "نسخه پدماوت", on "رای رتن سین مرزبان چتور[OCR?]"; the rest is lost: "[illegible] زوجه خود و [illegible] سلطان علاءالدین [illegible]" (p. 583).
  7. "نسخه راجاولی": "مصر بدیا دهر" wrote the rajas' names "بخط هندوی", and "ساهو رام" put it into Persian (p. 582).
  8. "نسخه راج ترنگینی", written in Sanskrit by "پندت رگهناته" and translated by "مولانائ عمادالدین" (p. 582).
  9. "تاریخ سلطان محمود غزنوی", by "مولانائے عنصری" (p. 582).
  10. "تاریخ سلطان شهاب الدین غوری" (p. 582).
  11. "تاریخ سلطان علاءالدین خلجی" (p. 582).
  12. "تاریخ فیروزشاهی تصنیف مولانائے ضیاءالدین" (p. 582).
  13. "تاریخ افاغنه", by "حسین خان افغان", who "سلسله جمیع افغانان به بنی اسرائیل برادران مهتر یوسف کنعانی رسانیده" (p. 582).
  14. "ظفرنامه", by "مولانائے شرف الدین علی یزدی" (p. 582).
  15. "تیمورنامه", by "مولانائ هاتفی", in verse (p. 582).
  16. "تاریخ بابری(۱)" (editor's footnote variant "واقعات بابری"), translated by "میرزا خان خانخانان عبدالرحیم" (p. 582).
  17. "اکبرنامه", by "شیخ ابوالفضل", tracing Akbar's line "تا حضرت ادم علیه السلام" (p. 582).
  18. "تاریخ اکبرشاهی تصنیف شیخ عطابک فروتنی[OCR?]" (p. 582).
  19. "اکبرنامه تصنیف شیخ الهدا و[OCR?] منشی مرتضی خان" (p. 582).
  20. "طبقات اکبری", by "خواجه نظام الدین احمد", "که جامع بسیارے تواریخ هندوستان است" (p. 582).
  21. "اقبال نامه جهانگیری", by "معتمد خان عرف محمد شریف" (p. 582).
  22. "جهانگیرنامه", by Jahangir himself (p. 582).
  23. "تاریخ شاهجهان تصنیف وارث خان", corrected by "علامی سعدالله خان" (p. 582).
  24. "تاریخ عالمگیری", by "میر محمد کاظم منشی", "لغایت ۱۰ ساله[OCR?] جلوس" (p. 582).
  25. "تاریخ کشمیری", covering "چهار هزار … سال", by "مولانائے شاه محمد شاه ابادی" (pp. 582–581).
  26. "تاریخ بهادرشاهی", on Gujarat Ahmadabad and "ولایت سند المشهور به تهته" (p. 581).
  27. "تاریخ سلاطین ولایت ملتان و مالوه و دولت آباد و دکن و جونپور و بنگاله و اودیسه" (p. 581).
  28. "دیگر تواریخ هند و فرس" (p. 581).
- Sources he names in the body:
  - For Akbar (pp. 252–253):
    - Khwaja Ata Beg Qazwini[OCR?], Khwaja Nizam al-Din Ahmad, Shaikh Ilahdad Faizi, "شیخ فرید المخاطب بمرتضی خان" and Muhammad Sharif Mutamad Khan.
    - Abul Fazl's three-daftar Akbarnama is praised above all.
    - There was no need to write Akbar's deeds, but he gives a summary for completeness.
  - Shah Tahmasp's farman "که در اکبر نامه داخل شده" (p. 142).
  - Muhammad Waris's "شاهجهان نامه" for Shah Jahan's reign, which would need "کتابے علیحده" (p. 102).
  - The "جهانگیرنامه" for the jugglers, recorded there "مفصل و مشروح" (p. 125).
  - Abul Fazl's elegy for Faizi "در اکبر نامه" (p. 166).
  - The "تاریخ سلطان بهادر شاه[OCR?]" for Sind (pp. 173–174), and "از تواریخ ولایت گجرات خصوص از تاریخ سلطان بهادر شاه والی انولایت" for Gujarat (p. 534).
  - "از بعضے تواریخ" for the Deccan (p. 161), Kashmir (p. 193), Multan ("از بعضے تواریخ بدین نمط بمطالعه این خاکسار آمده", p. 298) and Aurangabad (p. 539).
  - "ظفرنامه و اکبرنامه" for Koh-i Jud, which he explains from Jud, a Janjuha chief (p. 514).
  - "بکتب سلف" for Peshawar's names (p. 424).
  - "بموجب کتب هندوان" for the Ajmer lake (p. 535).
- Sources set against each other:
  - Amir Khusrau's "قران السعدین" shows that Bughra Khan came to conquer Delhi and his son set out to repel him, against the book's own tale of a loving meeting (p. 376).
  - On Prithviraj: "نسخه راجاولی و راج ترنگنی" (p. 433), "دفتر سویم اکبرنامه" (p. 432) and "تواریخ فارسی", closing "باختلافات بسیار ظاهر مے شود والله[OCR?] اعلم بالصواب" (p. 430).
  - On Bikramajit's death: the Akbarnama against the *Rajavali* and *Rajtarangini* (p. 444). "چنانچه در ولادت بکرماجیت اختلاف بسیار است هم چنان در مردن او نیز اختلاف دارد" (p. 442).
  - He follows the "سنگهاسن بتیسی" against the "اکبرنامه و دیگر تواریخ" for Bikramajit (p. 457).
- Gaps he admits:
  - Kangra's founding date is "در هیچ نسخه اندراج نیافته" (p. 123).
  - The details of Bisrwa's line are not "مفصل ظاهر" (p. 463).
  - From Raja Asmand on he rejects oral report as "شایان اعتبار ندانسته" and gives names and reigns only, from "بعضے نسخه" (p. 465).
  - The Indus's source is "ظاهر نیست", so he gives the travellers' report (p. 511).
- How he marks hearsay:
  - "گویند": Ayaz's origin (p. 410), Mahmud's deathbed (p. 407), Shihab al-Din's treasure (p. 401), Iltutmish's name (p. 399), the necklace (p. 423), Nanda's army (p. 415), Chandrai's elephant (p. 416), the Somnath idol (p. 413), the halala story (p. 386), the Fakhra anecdote (p. 392), the leather coins (p. 287), the Langah story (p. 525), the Punjab flood (p. 523) and Nanak's birth (p. 520).
  - "بقولی پنجاه سالگی" (p. 293).
  - "بعضی شعرا نوشته اند" (p. 408); "مولانا زلالی" and "محمود نامه" (p. 409).
  - "درکتب سلف اهل هند مرقوم است" (p. 413); "باعتقاد اهل هند" (pp. 417, 416).
  - "نسبت میدهند" and "دربعضی تواریخ" for Lahore's founding and names (p. 525).
  - "بعضی گویند" for Farid's studies in Qandahar and Sistan (p. 526) and the liver-eater's trance (p. 530).
  - "در باستانی نامهاے" for Bhakkar = Mansura (p. 525).
  - "بسیار از بسیار نوشته اند" for Ujjain's former size (p. 536).
  - "از سیاحان ممالک چنان استماع یافته" (p. 550).
  - Cross-references back within the book: "که سابقاً بتحریر درامده" and "چنانچه تفصیل آن بقلم درآمده" (p. 401); "که شمه از احوال او بتحریر درآمده" (p. 404).
- What he saw himself:
  - Of the liver-eaters: "اسیب این خدا ناترسان به چشم خود دیدم" (p. 529).
  - He paid a Baijnath hundi: "برنگارنده این صحیفه زنار داری هندوی بیجناتهه آورده بود سعادت خود دانسته ادائے زر کرده". He calls it "امری عجیب … که باعث حیرت ظاهر بینان میتواند بود" (p. 544).
  - He visited the Panjur[OCR?] garden in spring (p. 554).
  - At Kabul: "بعضی زنان را دیدم" who had left one husband for another and had up to fifteen or twenty husbands in their lives (p. 503).
  - He claims to have seen bhang-eaters fall "از صاحب دولتے بمفلسی", the habit passing to their descendants (pp. 462–461).

**On religion, saints and dervishes**
- On religions (pp. 587–586):
  - God brought into being "مذاهب متنوعه و مشارب مختلفه" just as He made the varied world (p. 587).
  - Each sect thinks the others' faith "محض ترهات", God's mercy its own, and harming other faiths pleasing to God. "این سجایای عامیان هر گروه است".
  - The "خاصان هر طایفه" hold mercy universal like sun and rain, live "با دوست همرنگ و با دشمن بی جنگ", and agree that "سر گروه هر گروه از تائیدات ایزدی … خالی نیست".
  - No one reaches the goal "بدون تحصیل و تکمیل علم" (p. 586).
- Kings, "ظلال ایزد متعال", must regard all God's servants alike, "مانند پرتو آفتاب که بهر نیک وبد یکسان می تابد" (p. 219).
- Akbar's faith:
  - He defends Akbar against Muslims "که خالی از تعصب نبودند": "بتقدیم شرایط اسلام واداء آداب بسادات عظام ... از دیگر سلاطین کم بظهور رسیده باشد" (p. 216).
  - On imitation: "از وزیدن[OCR?] تندباد[OCR?] تقلید چراغ امتیاز افسرده شده" (p. 218).
- Saint over prince: he puts Prince Muhammad Sultan's death down to his enmity with Shaikh Sadr al-Din, son of Bahauddin Zakariya (p. 385).
- The Gulistan and Bustan spread in India through the copies Sa'di sent to Multan: "ازان وقت هر دو کتاب متبرکه[OCR?] در هندوستان رواج یافت" (p. 386).
- How he grades sainthood:
  - Reported: Shaikh Mina is one "که مردم را گمان ولایت بدوست" (p. 545).
  - Asserted: Shaikh Muhammad Ghaus "در زمان خود در رتبه ولایت داشت" (p. 549), and Shah Madar "از اولیائے بزرگ هند بود" (p. 548).
  - Twice he says the saints' tombs in suba Delhi and around the city are past counting (pp. 554, 556).
  - Having finished "امکنه شریفه اولیائے مسلمین", he turns to "اماکن هنود" (p. 554).
- Dipaliwal: Hindu custodianship persists because "چون نظر خاص انحضرت بر دیپالی مذکور بود پیش نمیرود" (p. 521).
- Nanak:
  - His son did not succeed "چون دولت معنوی نصیب او نبود" (p. 520).
  - The devotion of Nanak's followers to their leader is rarely found among other groups (p. 519).
- The dervishes of Hind, in Sufi vocabulary (pp. 567–566):
  - Sannyasis are "صاحبان معارف و حقایق".
  - Jogis are "صاحب حال و قال".
  - Bairagis are "سالکان مسالک حقیقت و رهروان راه طریقت", with "سجاده", "تسبیح و تهلیل", "ذکر حضرت جل شانه" and "مراقبه".
  - The Udasi, Baba Nanak's believers, are ranked among the "درویشان هند".
  - The inexpressibility motif: "داستان دراز میشود" (p. 567); "تفصیل ان بتحریر درنیاید" (p. 571).
- The Jati and Sarpura (p. 566):
  - He calls them "این بدکیش".
  - They do not accept a Creator ("آفریننده مخلوقات را قبول نکنند"), and hold that men and animals reproduce of themselves from eternity, "چنانچه گیاه خود بخود می روید".
  - They take no account of punishment in the hereafter.
  - Their account of man as "مجموعه چهار عنصر" breaks off.
  - The Brahmins' saying: walk towards a rampaging elephant rather than towards a "سرپوره" (p. 565).
- Equations:
  - Dharm shastra "را در فارسی فقه گویند" (p. 570).
  - Vedanta's knowers are "صاحب توحید": "هرچند عالم از وست اما همه اوست" (p. 570).
  - The Upanishad concerns "توحید واحد مطلق" (p. 571).
  - Vedanta is unequalled in "علم الهی و مناظره", glossed with the verse "بحر یکی موج هزاران هزار" (p. 481).
  - "ارباب فرس" trace astrology to the Torah and Gospel, the people of Hind to the sun or the Veda (p. 569).
  - The Arabs fixed these rules well, but Hindi scholars have worked hard on them ("چهند") (p. 569).
  - Some Indian flowers "در ایران و توران و اکناف ان پیدا نیابد" (p. 575).
- The Indian learned's canon (p. 481):
  - What Vyasa put into circulation is authoritative and brings reward: "سمرت", "پوران", "گیان", and works of wisdom and astronomy.
  - What others "از خود اختراع کرده اند" is "شایان اعتبار ... نیست".
- The Mahabharata:
  - It is "تاریخی است بزرگ تر و از جمیع صحف معتبرتر". By Hindu histories, rule of Hindustan lay with the Pandavas and Kauravas from the creation (p. 500).
  - It has "ده باب" and "یک لک اشلوک", of which "هشتاد و شش هزار اشلوک" deal with religion, ethics and stories and "بست و چهار هزار اشلوک" with battles (p. 483).
  - Its name: "مها بزرگ و معنی بهارت جنگ است", or from Raja Bharat (p. 483).
- The four yugas "بموجب کتب معتبره و صحف قدیم" of Indian scholars (p. 480), figures as printed:
  - "ست جگ هفده لک و بست و هشت هزار سال", lifespan "یک لک سال".
  - "تریتا جگ دوازده لک و نود و شش هزار سال", "دهم حصه" remaining, lifespan "ده هزار سال".
  - "دواپر هشت لک و شصت و چهار هزار سال", "نه حصه زایل می گردد", lifespan "هزار سال".
  - "کلجگ چهار لک و سی و دو هزار سال", "دهم حصه" remaining, lifespan "صد سال"; the worst age.
- Earlier peoples' deeds seem superhuman only to this age (pp. 480–481). The Pandavas lived "در اواخر دور دواپر" (p. 480).
- Arjun's feats belong to the angels and are not remarkable in one "ملک نژاد" (p. 497).
- Reign totals (p. 478):
  - "ایام سلطنت پاندوان و کوروان مدت یک صد و بست و پنج سال";
  - joint rule "هفتاد و شش سال";
  - "درجودهن[OCR?] بعد اخراج پاندوان سیزده سال";
  - "راجه جدهشتر[OCR?] بعد جنگ مهابهارت سی و شش سال".
- Only a ruler of great deeds and justice has his accession date kept. Before Bikramajit only Yudhishthira's era survived (p. 445).

**Testing the marvellous: against credulity and against bigotry**
- Jwalamukhi: he calls those who proposed a sulphur mine "اهل تعصب". Jahangir's test and Firuz Shah's showed no mine (pp. 121–122; "بعضے اهل تعصب", p. 341).
- The jogi's body-transfer (p. 443):
  - "عقلای صائب رای آن را قبول نمی دارند".
  - The soul does not age, so an old body would not grow young because the jogi entered it.
  - Had the jogi entered the raja's body, people would have gone on calling him Raja Bikramajit, not Samandarpal.
  - "حکایت خلع بدن جوگی فروغی[OCR?] از صدق ندارد محض اراجیف است".
  - Either the raja died naturally or Salbahan killed him, and the jogi took the throne with the nobles' agreement; or else the jogi did away with him.
- Samandarpal traded "درویشے را ببادشاهی" and "خداطلبی را بدنیاطلبی" (p. 441).
- The Jalaliya rock (pp. 511–510):
  - Against the popular explanation ("برزبان عوام الناس است") he gives what he heard ("وجه تسمیه اینچنان استماع یافته").
  - An Afghan named Jalaliya was notorious under Akbar. When the royal jewel-boat broke on the rock, the emperor said "این سنگ هم جلالیه گردید".
  - Because "سخن بادشاهان بادشاه سخن است", the name stuck.
- Tirbeni: "اما در کتب هند برامدن سرستی ازانجا مندرج نیست" (p. 548).
- The Ganga's source: "هیچکس واقف نیست اما اعتقاد اهل هند انکه از بهشت نازل میشود" (p. 552).
- The jugglers' art is "سیمیا", common "در میان فرنگ" (p. 125).
- The rumour about Shah Mansur's death: "اگرچه برزبانها افتاده بود که راجه تودرمل بانی این منصوبه شده اما خلاف است شاید مردم دیگر اختراع کرده باشند" (p. 201).

**On places and peoples**
- Lahore: "بے شایبه تکلف شهریست بزرگ ... وسعت و آبادی و انبوهی مردم مانند این مصر کم نشان دهند" (p. 524).
- Sialkot's founding: two traditions side by side, without choosing, Shalkot/Raja Shal[OCR?] (citing the Mahabharat) and Salkot/Raja Salbahan (p. 517).
- The Chenab: the Hindi-book name is "چندر بهاگا", and "آب چناب و آب حیات تجنیس خطی" (p. 512).
- Multan:
  - The book's periodization of "ولایت ملتان": Muhammad Qasim; Mahmud; Ghuri; the Delhi sultans until "۸۵۵ [OCR?]"; independence under Sultan Muhammad Shah; Kamran's recovery (p. 298).
  - Shaikh Yusuf was "ساده لوح از مکر و خداع غافل", and Rai Sanhra[OCR?] "آن مکار" (p. 297).
  - Sultan Mahmud Langah kept a court of "اوباش و اجلاف" (p. 295). The child Husain's reign was "محض برای اسم" (p. 294).
  - Of the seasonal set-pieces framing Mahmud's campaigns (pp. 423–418), only the summer piece ends in a statement about a place: Multan's heat (pp. 420–419).
- The Baluch:
  - They keep their land safe by obedience and peshkash (p. 526).
  - Todar Mal's grant made their country a "یزک محکم" between Khurasan and Hindustan (p. 525).
  - Dipalpur's rebels are beyond the royal amirs' reach (p. 526).
- Kabul: "خردمندان" judge it "دروازه هند", and uniting Hind and Kabul under one ruler secured Punjab and all Hind (p. 501).
- Afghans and Hazaras:
  - He reports their own claims of descent as theirs ("میشمارند" / "قرار دهند") (p. 502).
  - His own judgement: "ادم دزد در ممالک دیگر کم خواهد بود و افغانان تمامی ادم دزد هستند" (p. 502).
- Kashmiris:
  - Their "ناپاکی" and "بد طینتی و نفاق سرشتی انہا", with a proverb and a scorpion-and-snake verse (p. 182).
  - "کشمیریان بد معاش زبون زیست هستند" (p. 183); "فریب و تزویر جبلی" (p. 188).
  - The land is a paradise, but its people are judged harshly (p. 121).
- Terrain explains unrest: the Rajputs' rebelliousness comes "از ریگستان" (p. 534), and Sorath's people "از افزونی درخت زار و درهم پیچیدگی کوهستان طریقه گردن کشی دارند" (p. 532).
- Delhi:
  - Abroad "دهلی" was the known name of the capital, and with Shahjahanabad all older sultans' cities took it, as rivers entering the Ganges take the name "گنگ" (p. 560).
  - "اردو گفتار که اصل زبان هندوستان از همین جاست" (p. 559).
  - No city matches Shahjahanabad, and Istanbul is not a tenth of a tenth of it (p. 557).
- Hindustan:
  - "هندوستان ملکیست وسیع … که ولایت دیگر بعشر عشیران نرسد" (p. 580).
  - Its rains are "سرامد تمامی بهارها", above the Nauroz spring of other lands (p. 579).
  - "انگور و خورپوزه … بهتر از ولایت می شود" (p. 578).
- "Turk": "مسلمین را در هند به همین سبب ترک مے گویند واز همین جهت حکام را نیز ترک مے نامند" (p. 427).

**On kingship, providence and rulers**
- Kingship:
  - When the age falls into disorder, divine wisdom places "افسر خلافت" on a fortunate head, who "احکام شریعت بکرسی نشاند" and clothes "برهنگان[OCR?] وادی احتیاج" (p. 73).
  - Kings are "ظلال حضرت آفریدگار", and "شریک ملک چون شریک حق محال است" (p. 79); "پریشان شود کار ملک از دو شاه" (p. 334).
- Providence:
  - Sovereignty "از کار فرمایان قضا و قدر در ازل مقرر کرده باشند" cannot be harmed (p. 279), and the same is said of Sher Shah's rise (p. 274).
  - "سلطنت نصیب خضر خان از ازل مقرر بود" (p. 328).
  - "سلطنت نه امریست که بسعی و تلاش شخصی و یا با مداد و اعانت کسی میسر تواند شد" (p. 293).
  - The Lodis' fall, "ارادت ازلی و مشیت لم یزلی" (p. 302), and Babur's rise, "مشیت ازلے بر آن رفته بود" (p. 307).
  - At "نراین": "چون مشیت قادر علی الاطلاق بران شد که سلسله جهانبانی هندوستان از فرقه هنود منقطع گردد" (p. 402).
  - God's court regards neither "حسب ونسب" nor "دین ومذهب", and His will ended the rule of the Hindus, "که از آغاز افرینش وارث سلطنت این مملکت هستند" (p. 429).
- Hardship before fortune:
  - "قانونی است قدیم" (p. 403) and "قانونی است مستمر" (p. 118): one near great fortune first suffers hardship.
  - "تقدیر دگر باشد و تدبیر دگر" (p. 99).
  - Every task has its appointed time, so "از محمد صالح کوتاهی بوقوع امده". Blocking the river at Siwistan would probably have caught Dara (p. 50).
- Fate cannot be undone:
  - "از آفریدها کیست که تقدیر آفریدگار را تواند رفع کرد" (p. 470).
  - Fate's arrow "از هفت سپر تدبیر انسان درگذرد" (p. 473).
  - Yudhishthira's losses at dice, despite "آن قدر عقل و دانش", are "تقدیر قادر مطلق" (p. 494).
  - The victory was decreed "در دیوان کده ازل", and Duryodhan got what his deeds deserved (p. 488).
- Rule and punishment:
  - Like a gardener thinning growth, a king disperses a crowd that gathers without need, "هرچند ناملایم از انها بظهور نرسد" (p. 371).
  - Death for "شرارت انگیزان مردم آزار" is "صلاح ملکی" (p. 379).
  - "اگر سلطان نفرماید سیاست   زند هر کس بخود لاف ریاست" (p. 417).
  - Crushing rebels "بر ذمت ہمت سلاطین والا اقتدار لازم است" (p. 262).
  - "از تلون طبیعت بادشاهان برحذر باید بود": kings may send the innocent "بشهرستان عدم" (p. 311).
  - A maxim "فرموده بزرگان": when luck turns, "نخستین عقل او تیره گردد" (p. 375).
- Kings who lost their thrones:
  - Through neglect, to a vizier: Khemin (p. 463), Adhast[OCR?] (p. 460), Budhmal[OCR?] with bhang (pp. 462–461).
  - Through pride: Raj Pal (p. 459).
  - Through opium: Sakwant (p. 458).
  - Through bad company and tyranny: Damodar Sen (p. 435).
  - Through youth and heedlessness: Jiwan Singh (p. 433).
- Essays:
  - Against music: "سرود و نغمه مردود اهل دلان پاک نهاد و مقبول ارباب فسق[OCR?]", oil on the fire of lust that raises "علم بت[OCR?] پرستی", aimed at Baz Bahadur (p. 243).
  - Tobacco: a mock eulogy turning to "استغفر الله چه می گویم و چه می نویسم", a list of harms (p. 134), and the note that punishment did not deter (p. 133).
  - Hair-dye (pp. 407–408):
    - He first praises dye as restoring youth's lustre.
    - Then, by the "قانون گویان اقلیم وجود", "عود جوانی بیرون از حیز امکانی است", and dyeing in old age is "محض ابلهی".
    - He closes with "پیری است نه کافری نهان نتوان کرد".
  - Bhang (pp. 462–461); opium: "کوکنار مذموم ترین کیفها است" (p. 458).
  - Wine, gambling, chess and ganjifa (p. 360); on gambling he cites "راجه نل و پاندوان" (p. 359).
  - Women, in verse and prose, around the Bharthari story (p. 453); a masnavi on the guile of women (p. 119).
- The war of succession and Aurangzeb:
  - Aurangzeb's march (p. 97):
    - He was moved by "حمیت دین مسلمانی و غیرت سلطنت و جهانبانی و رشک برادری و جوشش نفسانی[OCR?]".
    - He meant to free Shah Jahan from Dara, who would otherwise rule unchecked and "دین مهنو[OCR?] در ابواج[OCR?] میدهد" (sense unclear).
  - Dara's religion is the first cause of the new reign (p. 98).
  - The throne was kept "بامانت" for Aurangzeb since eternity, and "تفاول نام نامی" is a proof (p. 89).
  - The victory was "محض [illegible] تایید ربانی", "بے منت سپاه و لشکر" (p. 53).
  - Dara:
    - "در باطن مغلوب رعب و هراس گشته"; his "افسون" worked on Shuja and on Khanjar Khan (p. 76).
    - "چون بهره از شجاعت و مردانگی نداشت" (p. 84); no knowledge of "قواعد پیکار" (p. 86).
    - He wore out his army in the heat, under "کوکب بخت سوز[OCR?] دارا شکوهی" (p. 88).
    - "بدعاقبت[OCR?]" (p. 50).
  - Shuja: "چون عقل معامله دان نداشت و سود از زیان نمیدانست" (p. 61).
  - Jaswant Singh fled "در باطن از خبث سریرت خویش" (p. 57).
  - The emperor set aside "رائے ظاهر بینان و کنکاش[OCR?] عاقبت گزینان" to march in the rains (p. 70).
  - "حضرت اعلی را کمال توجه بحال خسران مال دارا شکوه بود" (p. 81).
- Jahangir's reign and Shah Jahan's revolt:
  - Mahabat Khan (pp. 115–112):
    - Divine will, "چشم زخمی", brought about "امری که از صلاح دور و بفساد نزدیک", and he was rebuked "بیموجب" at Nur Jahan's and Asaf Khan's request (p. 115).
    - Asaf Khan showed "نهایت غفلت و عدم احتیاط" (p. 114) and was the man "که این همه شورش ازو بود" (p. 112).
  - The emperor was "مست باده عشق و اسیر دام محبت نورجهان بیگم" and twice put his hand to his sword (p. 113).
  - The amirs saw Dawar Bakhsh as a "گوسپند قربانی" (p. 107).
  - The coincidence at the Bihat (p. 110), and the footman's fall read as the angel of death (p. 108).
  - Shah Jahan's "بغی[OCR?]" is blamed on "بد اندیشان" and "فتنه سازی کوته اندیشان و اغوای نورجهان بیگم", against a son "که غیر از اطاعت و فرمان پذیری امری دیگری نداشت" (p. 119).
  - Sher Afgan: "درین صورت عجب نیست که شیر افگن بموجب امر والا بقتل رسیده باشد" (p. 141). Side by side, the narrative on p. 142 has him killed by Qutb al-Din Khan's men in revenge.
  - Ghiyas Beg: "در کار کشائے اهل حاجات و رشوت گیری[OCR?] بے نظیر بود" (p. 141).
  - Nur Jahan: "رفته رفته کار بجائے رسید که از بادشاه جز نامی نماند" (p. 140).
  - Khan Alam: "ایلچی گری که او کرده دیگرے نکرده باشد در ایران اهل هند را نیک نام کرده آمد" (p. 138).
  - The highway trees: "اگرچه رسته درختان در شاهراه اختراع شیر خان افغان است اما در عهد دولت حضرت خاقان زمان رواجے تازه یافت" (p. 136).
  - Khusrau:
    - Flatterers ("صحبت خوشامد گویان کور باطن") fed his ambition.
    - The late emperor's words that Salim "عیش دوست است قابلیت سلطنت ندارد" and Khusrau "قابل خلافت" lodged in him as "ماخولیا[OCR?]" (p. 147).
    - He was a "شاهزاده نادان" (p. 146). Shah Jahan's part in his death is only rumour, "بر زبانها افتاد" (p. 144).
  - Salim at Allahabad showed "آثار بغی و گردن تابی", yet "ارباب غرض سخنان دور از کار از جانب شاهزاده بعرض ... رسانیدند" (p. 152).
- Akbar's reign:
  - Shaikh Mubarak's accusers acted "از روۓ غایت عداوت و عناد" (p. 155).
  - Abu'l-Fazl:
    - A long eulogy, wishing he had lived his span or died in notable service (p. 153).
    - "اگر درین وقت شیخ نرسیدے آن ولایت که بتلاش بدست آمده بود از دست میرفت" (p. 165).
    - Ahmadnagar was won "بشمشیر همت علامی شیخ ابوالفضل" (pp. 157, 162).
    - His "جوانمردی و شجاعت ذاتی" (p. 156).
  - Burhan al-Mulk: "کافران نعمت زود بپاداش کردار میرسند", his death "مکافات اعمال" (p. 165).
  - The Achal punishment was "بمقتضاۓ عدالت گستری", with verses on care of subjects (p. 164).
  - The poets and musicians:
    - Tansen: "بے شایبه اغراق در فن موسیقی و نغمه هندی از آغاز آفرینش تا حال مثل او کم کسے بود" (pp. 167–168).
    - Urfi: matchless in verse "اما خیلے متکبر و خودستا بود" (p. 167).
    - Faizi: "در شعر متین و سخن رنگین ... نظیر خود نداشت" (p. 167).
  - Todar Mal:
    - "در دقایق سیاق و حقایق حساب بے نظیر" (pp. 179–181).
    - His rules are still in force "تا حال", and later wazirs "تا حال میکوشند" to replace them and fail (p. 179).
  - The Khankhanan showed courage, wisdom and "فنون تدابیر صایبه" (p. 174).
  - Kashmir's sultans:
    - Sikandar: "سلطان خیلی متعصب بود" (p. 192).
    - Zain al-Abidin: "عدالت ورز و انصاف[OCR?] دوست" (p. 191).
    - When Abd al-Momin's insolent letter sank with the envoy's boat, people said it was perhaps "باشاره انحضرت", "و عجب نیست که چنین بوقوع آید" (p. 195).
  - Hakim Mirza's death from drink, "مبادا خردمند غرق شراب" (p. 200); harsh officials, "نباشے[OCR?] بکار جهان سخت گیر" (p. 201).
  - Bengal outside Delhi's control "از ابتداے ۷۴۶[OCR?] لغایت سنه مذکور [۹۸۳[OCR?]] که دوصد وسی وهفت سال بود باشد" (p. 206).
  - Loyalty: verses against betraying the "صاحب نمک"/"ولی نعمت" (p. 232). Khan Zaman's revolt came from "صحبت بد ذاتان" (p. 234).
  - Sultan Muzaffar acted "از روسے بد قولی و بد طینتی" (p. 225).
  - The emperor's two-sided verdict on Ahmadabad: hell in climate, paradise in its people (p. 229).
  - Bairam Khan took the emperor for "خورد سال" and killed Tardi Beg Khan without orders (p. 247).
  - Abu al-Maali: "حیف باشد کہ نکو روی نکو خوے نبود" (p. 237).
- The Afghans:
  - Sher Shah and Islam Shah ruled as few past sultans had (p. 260).
  - Sher Shah was "بغایت نیک ذات و فرخنده صفات", "بے نظیر" in statecraft, and "گوے سبقت از نوشیروان عادل برده" (p. 267). The bania's wife anecdote: "نزدیک من در عدالت فرزند و رعیت برابر است" (pp. 266–267).
  - Islam Shah was "در رعیت پروری و عدالت گستری ... بے نظیر", but kept the soldiery "بغایت تنگ و ازرده" (p. 263).
  - Sher Khan judged the Mughals easy to drive out, because the emperor left affairs to wazirs and was given to the hunt and pleasure. The Afghans' fault is that "با خود اتفاق[OCR?] ندارند" (p. 272).
  - Kamran's humiliation was deserved: "چرا نگردد که از قبله و کعبه خود روگردان گشته بدشمن جانی التجا اورده بود" (p. 261).
  - Hemu was ugly and short and could not ride, yet "آنچه او کرده دیگرسے نکرده باشد" (p. 258).
  - Reign lengths as printed: Islam Shah "هشت سال و دو ماه و هشت روز" (p. 259); Adil "قریب دو سال", and "چهار تن شانزده سال" from Sher Shah to Adil (p. 257); Humayun "مرتبه اول ده سال و مرتبه دویم ده ماه" (p. 254).
- Humayun:
  - "اینهمه شکست از غفلت و بی پروای آنحضرت بوقوع آمد" (p. 288); ill fortune darkens the mind (p. 289).
  - Kamran's "حسد فطری و عداوت جبلی" (p. 287).
  - In the emperor's words, Hindustan was lost "از بیوفائی ہمراہان و بے التفاتی برادران" (p. 281).
- The Lodis and Babur:
  - "از بدسلوکی و بیدادی سلطان ابراهیم اکثر امرا بر گشت" (p. 304).
  - Ibrahim was young and acted without his ministers (p. 312). He killed Mian Bhuwa "ناحق", "باغواۓ بداندیشان" (p. 311), and his brother "از روۓ بیرحمی", with a verse against it (p. 312).
  - Panipat was "فتحی که بادشاهان پیشین را میسر نشده بود و مقدمه تمام فتوحات هندوستان تواند شد" (p. 301).
  - In Babur's speech before Rana Sanga: "اگر ... فتح مے شود غازی مے شویم و اگر کشته میگردیم درجه شهادت مے یابیم" (p. 299).
  - Sikandar Lodi:
    - "سلطان خیلے متعصب بود".
    - "اول کسے[OCR?] که از سلاطین هندوستان هندوان را خوار و نزار داشته و بدعوی اسلام برهمن را کشته".
    - Yet "خیلے هوشیار و صاحب دانش" (p. 314), with a just reign and cheap grain (p. 315).
  - Bahlul:
    - His taking of Badaun was "بیوفائے و بیمروتی" (p. 316).
    - He is otherwise praised for the sharia, justice and his ulama and fuqara (p. 316).
    - He was "قوی اقبال و بیدار بخت" (p. 317).
  - Ala al-Din: "از بے همتی انتقام نگرفت" (p. 319); "غیرت بخاطر نیاورده" (p. 322).
- The sultans of Delhi:
  - Iqbal Khan's end was "نتیجه حرام نمکی و عهد شکنی" (p. 329).
  - The Malwa peace was "باعث زبونی و بد نقشی سلطان" (p. 324).
  - Ala al-Din was "از پدر هم[OCR?] … سست تر است" (pp. 323–324).
  - Hisam Khan's counsel is endorsed in a verse (p. 323).
  - Dynastic counts (p. 329):
    - the Tughluqs, "هشت تن مدت نود و شش سال و دو ماه و یازده روز";
    - the Turks, "بست و چهار تن دوصد و سی و سه سال".
  - Muhammad Shah:
    - "از عجایب مخلوقات و جامع اضداد" (pp. 350–351).
    - "سلطان بے رحم و آهن دل" (p. 346); his hill plan was an "اراده فاسد" (p. 347).
    - "خلایق از ظلم او نجات یافت" (p. 345).
  - Firuz Shah: "سیاست را مطلق ترک داد هیچ مسلمانی را آزار نداد" (p. 339); "الناس علی دین ملوکهم" (p. 340).
  - Qutb al-Din raised a low-born favourite to the wazirate (p. 358).
  - Tughluq's death: he calls the Nizam al-Din account "انچه تحقیق پیوسته" (p. 351).
  - Ala al-Din's new religion was "خیالات فاسده", which Ala al-Mulk argues would bring "خرابی ملک و سلطنت" (p. 367).
  - Jalal al-Din's clemency is praised with verse (pp. 372–373).
  - Bughra Khan: "در اصل ناصرالدین از سلطنت بے نصیب بود و اثار ناقابلیت ازو هویدا" (p. 384).
  - Balban:
    - Against rebels he "خدا پرستی را بگوشه گذاشته کمال جباری وقهاری کردی" (p. 390).
    - Of the Katehriya massacre: "گنه بود مردم ستمکاره را / چه تاوان زن وطفل بیچاره را" (p. 387).
    - He would not campaign far from Delhi because the Mongols raided Punjab (p. 388).
  - Iltutmish's sons: "سابقا از غفلت وبے پروائی پسران سلطان شمس الدین در جمیع امور جهانبانے اختلال رو داد" (p. 388).
  - Shah Turkan: "بمقتضاۓ کوته اندیشی که در سرشت زنان مخمر است" (p. 398).
  - Raziya: "در شجاعت وعدالت از مردان بهره وافی داشت" (p. 397).
- Mahmud of Ghazni:
  - He campaigned "بمقتضائے مراسم دین داری بقصد جهاد" (p. 424), with "حمیت دین" at Multan (p. 420) and "بقصد جهاد" at Thanesar (p. 417).
  - "ازانجا که سلطان قوے اقبال بود بهر طرف که روے عزیمت می کرد فتح ونصرت استقبال می نمود" (p. 420).
  - Bije Rai acted "از روے بے همتی و بے تدبیری" (p. 421).
  - Verses mock the idols (p. 416), set Kalchand's suicide against "زیستن چون بکام خصم بود" (p. 416), and praise humble prayer, "کین تضرع را برحق قدرها ست" (p. 415).
  - Verses on giving while one can, at his death (p. 406).
  - Following "بعضی شعرا", the Sultan loved Ayaz for his character, not his face (p. 408).

**The 1918 editor's remarks (kept apart from the author)**
- Authorship:
  - The text never names its author: p. 13; "حتی که اوس کے نام کا بھی پته نہیں چلتا" (p. 600).
  - Only the endorsements of MSS II, III and IV give "Sujan Rai Bhandari of Batala" (p. 13). Side by side, the MS 3 colophon he quotes contains no name (p. 601).
  - Dowson read the name as "Subhan Rai" and Batala as "Patyala" (p. 13); "سبحان رائے" and "پٹیاله" (p. 600).
  - From the Hindu eras he presumes the author a Hindu (pp. 13, 600).
- Why the author hid his name:
  - He was "محض ایک معمولی اور غیر معروف شخص" whose aim was "اظہار حقیقت حال ... جو ایک مورخ کا نصب العین ہونا چاہیے".
  - So his statements are "رطب و یابس سے پاک اور اصل واقعات پر مبنی", especially for Aurangzeb's time, "جبکه وه اکثر واقعات کا خود شاہد متصور ہے" (p. 599).
  - In English: he takes the concealed name as a sign of an unbiased writer (p. 13).
- Date and scope:
  - "in the 40th[OCR?] year of the reign of Aurangzeb, corresponding to 1107[OCR?] A. H. (1695-6[OCR?] A. D.)" (p. 13).
  - "سنه ۴۰ جلوس اورنگ زیب مطابق سنه ۱۱۰۷ ہجری (سنه ۱۶۹۵-۹۶ع)", over "دو سال" (p. 599).
  - Side by side, the regnal year on p. 602 is "[illegible: a superscript numeral]".
  - "chiefly a history of Delhi" (p. 13); "یه کتاب مخصوص دہلی کی تاریخ ہے", with other kingdoms treated where they were joined to Delhi (p. 599).
  - Three parts (pp. 599, 14).
- Value:
  - Part 1 "بہت زیاده قابل قدر ہے", "گویا که یه حصه آئین اکبری کا ایک ضمیمه ہے" (pp. 599–598); "the most valuable portion", "supplementing the Ain-i-Akbari" (p. 14).
  - The Punjab, suba Lahore and pargana Batala rest on "مصنف کی ذاتی واقفیت" (p. 598; p. 14).
  - Part 2's events "عموماً قصه و کہانی سے زیاده وقعت نہیں رکھتے" (p. 598).
  - Part 3 is largely borrowed, except the civil war, which is "مستند" (p. 598; p. 14).
  - The first history of the Muslim kings "ایک ہندو کے قلم سے", not written "پادشاه وقت کے زیر اثر" (p. 598); "not composed under the court influence" (p. 14).
- How the edition was made:
  - The editor learned of the book from "آثار الصنادید" and was helped by Hailey and Marshall (pp. 604–603; p. 9).
  - Five manuscripts were collated, with variants in the footnotes and the text marked "ن" (p. 603). The English introduction prints the mark as "ب[OCR?]" (p. 10).
- The manuscripts:
  - MS 1's colophon is dated year "احد[OCR?]" of Alamgir II, "یکهزار و یکصد و شصت و هشت هجری". The editor calls it the "زیاده مستند" copy (pp. 602–601).
  - MS 2's colophon reads "سنه یکهزار و دو صد چیزے بالا هجری" and "سمت راجه بکرماجیت یکهزار و هشت صد شصت و چهار" (p. 601).
  - MS 3's date is rubbed out (p. 601).
  - MS 4 is undated (p. 600).
  - MS 5 is incomplete: "دیکھو صفحه ۴۹۰" (p. 600).
- The appended notice of Aurangzeb's death, dated "سنه ۱۱۱۸[OCR?] هجری", is a copyist's addition in the editor's and Dowson's view (p. 602; p. 10).
- He regrets the lithograph's errors (p. 597) and the old scribes' freedom "نقطوں اور مرکز دینے[OCR?] کی پابندی سے" (p. 598).
- Editor's footnotes in the body:
  - They check the text against "تاریخ فرشته" and "طبقات اکبری" (pp. 157–161; pp. 172–173 with "آئین اکبری"; pp. 188–193 with "تاریخ فرشته جلد دوم"), and against "تاریخ فرشتہ جلد اول" (pp. 311, 313, 316) and "فرشته جلد اول" (pp. 325–327).
  - They also cite "اکبر نامہ" (pp. 177, 305, 309) and "اقبال نامہ[OCR?]" (p. 182).
  - Variant names include "راجه سیه دیو" / "راجه سری دیو" for Sahdev and "کوکتا[OCR?] دیوی" for Kota Devi (p. 193).
  - On Shaikh Yusuf, "فذالک [OCR?] سنه ۸۵۰ [OCR?] است [illegible] تاریخ فرشته جلد دوم صفحہ [illegible] و [illegible] اکبری صفحہ [illegible] [illegible]" sets 850 against the text's "۸۵۵ [OCR?]" (p. 297).
  - On Tegh Bahadur's death, "سنه هجری با سنه جلوس مطابقت[OCR?] ندارد سنه هفدهم جلوس عالمگیری موافق سنه ۸۴[OCR?] - ۸۵[OCR?] هجری می باشد" (p. 519).
  - All of these are the editor's, not the author's.

## Figures and sites not in the archive

*Merged from the "Other saints, sites and events" sections of chunks 001-025 (first half; chunks 026-048 follow under the same subsections). Page references are PDF pages; this book cites no folios. Index locators are quoted as text, never as pages. Names, dates and places are as Pass 1 printed them, with every flag. A `[PK? — book's place: …]` tag means the bullet is placed in present Pakistan only because the book names that place.*

### Muslim saints, dervishes, shrines and khanqahs

*Chunks 001-025:*

- **Index headwords, chunk 001 (indexed only):** "موسیٰ گیلانی" — ۶۱ (p. 19), not mapped (see Doubts); "نصیرالدین محمود چراغ دہلوی" — ۳۳[OCR?], ۶۲[OCR?] (p. 18); "وجیہ الدین محمدؒ" — ۶۱[OCR?] (p. 17); "میرحسینی" — ۶۱ (p. 19).
